Strings in Java
Strings are one of the most important topics in Java interviews. This
guide covers the fundamentals, memory behavior, common methods,
character conversions, char[], StringBuilder, and DSA examples.
1. What Is a String in Java?
A String is a sequence of characters. In Java, String is a
class, not a primitive data type.
String name = "Java";
String str = new String("Hello");
Modern Java internally stores string data using a byte array with
encoding information. Earlier Java versions used a character array.
2. Why Are Strings Immutable?
Immutable means that once a String object is created, its value
cannot be changed.
String s = "Hello";
s.concat(" World");

System.out.println(s); // Hello
concat() creates and returns a new String; it does not modify the
original object.

To retain the concatenated value, assign the result:
String s = "Hello";
s = s.concat(" World");

System.out.println(s); // Hello World
Why did Java make Strings immutable?
1. Security: Strings are commonly used for file paths, URLs,
   database connections, and other sensitive values.
2. Thread safety: Immutable objects can be shared safely between
   threads.
3. String Pool: Immutability makes it safe to reuse string
   literals.
4. Hash code caching: Strings are often used as keys in HashMap;
   an unchanged value means its hash code remains stable.
3. String Pool
Java maintains a special area for interned strings, commonly called the
String Constant Pool.
String s1 = "Java";
String s2 = "Java";

System.out.println(s1 == s2); // true
Both references point to the same pooled string object.

Creating strings with new is different:
String s1 = new String("Java");
String s2 = new String("Java");

System.out.println(s1 == s2);      // false
System.out.println(s1.equals(s2)); // true
Why?
- new String() explicitly creates a distinct String object.
- == compares object references.
- equals() compares string contents.
What does intern() do?
intern() returns the canonical pooled representation of a string.
String s1 = new String("Java");
String s2 = s1.intern();
String s3 = "Java";

System.out.println(s2 == s3); // true
4. String vs StringBuilder vs StringBuffer
  Feature           String            StringBuilder     StringBuffer
  Mutable           No                Yes               Yes

  Thread safety     Safe to share     Not synchronized  Synchronized
because it is                       methods
immutable                           

  Repeated          Creates new       Efficient         Usually slower
modifications     strings                             than
StringBuilder
Synchronization   Not needed for    No                Yes, at method
immutability                        level
Example
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Hello");

sb.append(" World");
sb.reverse();

System.out.println(sb); // dlroW olleH
Use:
- String: When the value does not need frequent modification.
- StringBuilder: For repeated modifications, typically within a
  single thread.
- StringBuffer: When synchronized mutable string operations are
  required.
5. Important String Methods
String s = "Hello World";

s.length();                  // 11
s.charAt(0);                 // 'H'
s.substring(0, 5);           // "Hello"
s.toLowerCase();             // "hello world"
s.toUpperCase();             // "HELLO WORLD"
s.contains("World");         // true
s.startsWith("Hello");       // true
s.endsWith("World");         // true
s.indexOf('o');              // 4
s.lastIndexOf('o');          // 7
s.replace("World", "Java");  // "Hello Java"
s.trim();                    // removes leading/trailing chars <= U+0020
s.isEmpty();                 // false
s.equals("Hello World");     // true
s.equalsIgnoreCase("hello world"); // true
Remember: substring(start, end) includes start but excludes
end.

trim() removes leading and trailing characters whose code point is at
most U+0020. For broader Unicode whitespace handling, consider strip()
(Java 11+).
6. String Comparison: == vs equals()
String s1 = "Java";
String s2 = "Java";
String s3 = new String("Java");

System.out.println(s1 == s2);      // true
System.out.println(s1 == s3);      // false
System.out.println(s1.equals(s3)); // true
- ==: Checks whether references point to the same object.
- equals(): Checks whether the string contents are equal.
7. Uppercase and Lowercase Conversions
Convert a String to lowercase
Use toLowerCase():
String str = "HELLO JAVA";
String result = str.toLowerCase();

System.out.println(result); // hello java
Convert a String to uppercase
Use toUpperCase():
String str = "hello java";
String result = str.toUpperCase();

System.out.println(result); // HELLO JAVA
Important: Strings are immutable. These methods return a new string;
they do not modify the original.

For locale-independent identifiers (for example, protocol keys), use
Locale.ROOT:
import java.util.Locale;

String key = "Java".toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT);
Convert an individual character
Use the Character class:
char ch = 'a';

char upper = Character.toUpperCase(ch);
char lower = Character.toLowerCase('B');

System.out.println(upper); // A
System.out.println(lower); // b
Useful Character methods:
  Method                              Purpose
  Character.toUpperCase(ch)         Convert a character to uppercase

  Character.toLowerCase(ch)         Convert a character to lowercase

  Character.isUpperCase(ch)         Check whether a character is
uppercase

  Character.isLowerCase(ch)         Check whether a character is
lowercase

  Character.isLetter(ch)            Check whether a character is a
letter

  Character.isDigit(ch)             Check whether a character is a
digit
Character.isLetterOrDigit(ch)     Check whether a character is a
letter or digit
ASCII-based conversion (useful for DSA)
For English ASCII letters, uppercase and lowercase character codes
differ by 32:

  Character     ASCII value
  A                    65
a                    97

Lowercase to uppercase:
char ch = 'a';
char upper = (char) (ch - 32);

System.out.println(upper); // A
Uppercase to lowercase:
char ch = 'A';
char lower = (char) (ch + 32);

System.out.println(lower); // a
Note: This arithmetic works only for English ASCII letters. Prefer
Character.toUpperCase() and Character.toLowerCase() in production
code. Unicode case conversion can be more complex than a
one-character-to-one-character mapping.
Check whether a character is uppercase or lowercase
char ch = 'A';

if (Character.isUpperCase(ch)) {
    System.out.println("Uppercase");
} else if (Character.isLowerCase(ch)) {
    System.out.println("Lowercase");
} else {
    System.out.println("Neither uppercase nor lowercase");
}
8. String and Character Array Conversions
Convert a String to char[]
Use toCharArray():
String str = "hello";
char[] arr = str.toCharArray();

for (char ch : arr) {
    System.out.println(ch);
}
Output:
h
e
l
l
o
Access individual characters:
String str = "Java";
char[] arr = str.toCharArray();

System.out.println(arr[0]); // J
System.out.println(arr[2]); // v
Convert char[] to String
Method 1: String constructor
char[] arr = {'J', 'a', 'v', 'a'};
String str = new String(arr);

System.out.println(str); // Java
Method 2: String.valueOf()
char[] arr = {'H', 'e', 'l', 'l', 'o'};
String str = String.valueOf(arr);

System.out.println(str); // Hello
9. StringBuilder in Java
StringBuilder is a mutable sequence of characters. Unlike String,
its contents can be modified.
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Hello");

sb.append(" Java");

System.out.println(sb); // Hello Java
Important StringBuilder methods
  Method             Example                    Purpose
  append()         sb.append("Hi")          Adds content at the end
insert()         sb.insert(0, "Hi")       Inserts content at an index
delete()         sb.delete(0, 2)          Deletes a range
deleteCharAt()   sb.deleteCharAt(0)       Deletes one character
reverse()        sb.reverse()             Reverses the sequence
replace()        sb.replace(0, 2, "Hi")   Replaces a range
charAt()         sb.charAt(0)             Returns the character at an index
setCharAt()      sb.setCharAt(0, 'A')     Updates a character
length()         sb.length()              Returns the length
toString()       sb.toString()            Converts to String

Remember: delete(start, end) and replace(start, end, str)
include start but exclude end.
Convert String to StringBuilder
String str = "Hello";
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder(str);

sb.append(" World");

System.out.println(sb); // Hello World
Convert StringBuilder to String
Use toString():
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Hello");
String str = sb.toString();

System.out.println(str); // Hello
Convert char[] to StringBuilder
char[] arr = {'J', 'a', 'v', 'a'};
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();

for (char ch : arr) {
    sb.append(ch);
}

System.out.println(sb); // Java
Alternatively:
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder(new String(arr));
Convert StringBuilder to char[]
StringBuilder does not provide a toCharArray() method. Convert it
through a String:
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Java");
char[] arr = sb.toString().toCharArray();
10. String vs char[] vs StringBuilder
  Feature            String                 char[]         StringBuilder
  Mutable            No                     Yes              Yes
Access by index    charAt()             arr[i]         charAt()
Update character   Not directly           arr[i] = 'A'   setCharAt()
Append             Creates a new String   Manual           append()
Reverse            No direct method       Manual           reverse()
11. DSA Interview Examples
Example 1: Reverse a String using StringBuilder
class Solution {
    public String reverseString(String s) {
        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder(s);
        return sb.reverse().toString();
    }
}
Time complexity: O(n)
Space complexity: O(n)
Example 2: Reverse a String using char[] and two pointers
class Solution {
    public String reverseString(String s) {
        char[] arr = s.toCharArray();

        int left = 0;
        int right = arr.length - 1;

        while (left < right) {
            char temp = arr[left];
            arr[left] = arr[right];
            arr[right] = temp;

            left++;
            right--;
        }

        return new String(arr);
    }
}
Time complexity: O(n)
Space complexity: O(n) for the character array.
Example 3: Remove a character from StringBuilder
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Hello");

sb.deleteCharAt(1);

System.out.println(sb); // Hllo
12. Important Interview Questions
Q1. Can we modify a String using charAt()?
No. charAt() only retrieves a character. To modify a character,
convert the String into a char[] or use StringBuilder.setCharAt().
Q2. Is StringBuilder thread-safe?
No. StringBuilder is not synchronized. StringBuffer provides
synchronized methods.
Q3. Does StringBuilder have a toCharArray() method?
No. Convert it through a String:
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("Java");
char[] arr = sb.toString().toCharArray();
Q4. Can we compare two StringBuilder objects using equals()?
StringBuilder does not override equals() to compare contents. It
inherits reference-based equality from Object.

To compare contents:
boolean same = sb1.toString().equals(sb2.toString());
13. Quick Interview Revision
- String is immutable; StringBuilder and StringBuffer are
  mutable.
- == compares references; equals() compares String contents.
- String literals can be reused through the String Pool.
- intern() returns the canonical pooled representation.
- Use toCharArray() to convert a String into a character array.
- Use new String(charArray) or String.valueOf(charArray) to create
  a String from a character array.
- Use StringBuilder for repeated string modifications in a single
  thread.
- Use Character methods for character-level case conversion and
  classification.
- For lowercase English letters, ch - 'a' gives an index from 0 to
  25, which is useful for frequency-array problems.
